
❯ Look at hw3 pdf document and help me write a basic READme with the plan of
  action that I am about to give you:
  #       Question        Points  What changes in the harness
  1       Pipelined?      10      Dependent vs. independent add chains
  2       Out-of-order?   10      Two independent cache-miss loads, with and
  without filler between them
  3       Branch prediction?      10      Predictable vs. random branch pattern
  15      Pipeline depth  10      Reuse Q3 data: cycles lost per mispredict
  14      Fetch width     10      NOPs of different byte lengths
  4       ROB size        30      Q2’s experiment, sweeping the amount of
  filler
  5       Physical register file size + class     35      Q4’s experiment with
  register-writing filler
  Total           115

  Why these:

  Q1, Q2, Q3 and Q15 are close to textbook experiments and are hard to get
  wrong.
  Q4 is a direct extension of Q2.
  Q5 is Q4 with different filler, so 35 points costs very little extra work.

  Why I’d avoid the others:

  TLB questions (6–9): inside SGX, enclave memory only uses 4 KB pages, so Q9
  can’t really be done in the enclave. EPC size limits also complicate TLB
  sweeps.
  BTB questions (10–12): modern chips have multi-level BTBs, so results are
  noisy.
  Q13 (predictor type): hard to conclude anything with confidence.
  Q16 (SMT sharing): needs SMT sibling control in the enclave too.

  Fallback: if Q5 turns out messy, Q8 (TLB miss cost) plus Q9 (page sizes) adds
  20 points. You’d use a pointer-chase you’ll already have from Q2/Q4, and
  report the enclave huge-page limitation.

  How each experiment works (short version)
  Q1 Pipelined. Time N dependent adds against N independent adds spread over
  several registers. If the independent version runs at more than 1/latency
  instructions per cycle, instructions overlap, so the processor is pipelined.
  Q2 OoO. Build a pointer chase over a buffer much larger than the last-level
  cache, so each load misses.
  Put two loads from independent chains back to back. If the time is about 1
  miss instead of 2, they overlapped, so execution is out-of-order.
  Q4 ROB size. Use the method from Henry Wong’s “Measuring reorder buffer
  capacity” blog post: miss, then N independent filler instructions (e.g.
  NOPs), then another independent miss.
  Plot time against N. The jump, where time roughly doubles, is where the
  second miss no longer fits in the ROB. That N is your ROB size estimate.
  Q5 PRF size. Same setup, but the filler writes registers, e.g. add rax, rbx.
  Avoid zeroing idioms like xor reg,reg and plain movs, because rename/move
  elimination means they may not use a physical register.
  The knee now appears where physical registers run out.
  Repeat with vector filler such as vaddps ymm. Different knees for integer and
  vector filler show the register classes are separate.
  Q3 Branch prediction. Run a loop containing a branch driven by an array.
  Compare an always-taken pattern, a short repeating pattern, and a random
  pattern.
  Random being much slower than patterned (and branch-misses going up, if perf
  works) shows the processor predicts branches.
  Q15 Pipeline depth. Compute (random time − predictable time) / number of
  mispredicts, giving cycles per mispredict (often ~15–20).
  Explain that this measures the front-end-to-resolution refill penalty, which
  is a lower bound on depth, not an exact stage count.
  Q14 Fetch width. Run long unrolled blocks of NOPs of 1, 4, 8 and 15 bytes.
  Instructions per cycle hits a ceiling for short NOPs; bytes per cycle hits a
  ceiling for long NOPs. The bytes-per-cycle ceiling (often 16 B/cycle for
  legacy decode) is the fetch estimate.
  Note the µop-cache caveat. A very large code footprint can push you off the
  µop cache.
  Measurement hygiene (put this in your method section once)
  Pin to one core with taskset, keep that core’s SMT sibling idle, and run 10
  or more repetitions. Report the median and the min/max or standard deviation.
  Converting rdtsc to cycles: rdtsc ticks at a fixed frequency, not core
  cycles.
  Calibrate with a long dependent 1-cycle add chain. That chain runs at 1
  add/cycle, so it gives you the TSC-to-cycle ratio.
  This also saves you if perf is blocked on the course machines
  (perf_event_paranoid), which is common.
  Use asm volatile or .S files so the compiler doesn’t optimize your filler
  away. Plan to show these as the “code snippet” in the report.
  The enclave requirement (the main risk)

  This is where most of your time will go, so start it early:

  Use Gramine-SGX (or similar) to run your unmodified binary in an enclave.
  This beats writing a native SGX SDK app for each experiment. Report the
  platform (SGX1 vs SGX2, Gramine version, EPC size, debug vs production
  enclave).
  Timing may not work inside the enclave:
  rdtsc may fault or be unavailable inside enclaves, depending on the CPU and
  SGX version.
  Performance counters are generally hidden from enclaves.
  Plan a fallback: run many iterations per enclave call and time the whole call
  from outside, so the call overhead is amortized. Check what your specific
  machine allows first.
  Memory size matters for Q2/Q4/Q5. The pointer-chase buffer must be bigger
  than the LLC but fit within usable EPC. Enclave cache misses also cost more
  because memory is encrypted. That’s a nice expected difference to discuss:
  the knee position in N should stay the same even though absolute times grow.
  If one machine has no SGX, that’s allowed. Just document it, and still run
  the ordinary experiments on two machines.
  Choosing machines: pick two with different microarchitectures (e.g. Skylake
  vs. Ice Lake), so the side-by-side comparison actually shows something. At
  least one must be SGX-capable. Record lscpu//proc/cpuinfo output for the CPU
  model.
  Working on TRACK B (AI use allowed)

● I updated README.md with both changes.

  - Artemisia entry:
    - It is a Xeon Gold 5420+ with 2 sockets, 28 cores per socket and 2 threads
      per core, which makes 112 logical CPUs across 2 NUMA nodes.
    - Frequency range is 800–2000 MHz. constant_tsc, nonstop_tsc and rdtscp are
      present, so rdtsc should work natively.
    - The toolchain is GCC 11.5, Python 3.9 and kernel 6.17.8.
  - PMU policy: a new "PMU policy" subsection says the first measurements use
    rdtsc timing only. PMU counters are used afterwards, only to verify those
    results.
  - SGX: Artemisia can't run enclaves. The kernel log says "SGX disabled or
    unsupported by BIOS", and without sudo you can't change that. Gramine is
    installed there, but gramine-sgx won't work. So your enclave runs have to
    come from the second machine, which must be SGX-capable, and Artemisia only
    gets ordinary runs. I noted that gramine-direct can serve as a non-SGX
    baseline there.
  - Microarchitecture: I wrote Sapphire Rapids (Golden Cove cores) from
    published specs, not from your output. Please confirm it against the lscpu
    family and model.
  - SMT siblings: I assumed they are CPUs n and n+56, based on the NUMA node
    ranges. Check this in
    /sys/devices/system/cpu/cpu*/topology/thread_siblings_list before pinning.

  Which machine do you plan to use as the second one? It needs SGX.

● I updated README.md to show SGX is now on for Artemisia. The SGX entry in the
  machine table is now "Yes", with the sgx and sgx_lc flags and the /dev/sgx_*
  devices as evidence.

  - Microarchitecture: lscpu reports family 6, model 143, stepping 8, which
    matches Sapphire Rapids. I added that to the table.
  - EPC: dmesg shows two sections, one per socket. They look like about 1 GiB
    each, but I didn't compute the exact total, so the README tells you to
    record it.
  - Max MHz: your two captures disagree, 2000 and 4100. I noted that and added a
    note to re-capture and record which value is right.
  - Second machine: Artemisia now supplies the SGX comparison, so the second
    machine only needs a different microarchitecture.

  Device permissions: /dev/sgx_enclave is root:sgx with mode 0660, and your
  account isn't in the sgx group. gramine-sgx will probably fail to open it, and
  without sudo you can't fix that yourself. I noted this as an open issue. Try
  a minimal gramine-sgx hello-world now, and ask the course admins to add you to
  the sgx group if it fails. /dev/sgx_provision is root:sgx_prv, so you only
  need that group if you do attestation.

  The README changed on disk after my last edit. I treated that as deliberate

       5 A dependent chain can never beat 1/latency instructions per cycl
         e. If the independent
       6 version runs faster than that, several instructions are in fligh
         t at once, so the
       7 processor is pipelined (and, for add, superscalar).

         .

       3 ## Idea
       4 Same loop as Q4, but every filler instruction **writes a registe
         r**. Every in-flight
       5 register write holds a physical register until it retires. If th
         e physical registers
       6 run out before the ROB does, the knee moves to a smaller N. The
         knee position then
       7 gives the number of *free* physical registers of that class.

       3 ## Idea
       4 Loop over an array and execute one conditional branch per elemen
         t, driven by the
       5 element's value. Both paths do the same work (one `add`), so the
          only thing that
       6 differs between patterns is how predictable the branch is.
       7
       8 - Always-taken / never-taken → trivially predictable.

       4 A mispredicted branch throws away everything fetched after it. F
         etch then restarts on
       5 the correct path, and the front end has to refill before useful
         work resumes. The time
       6 lost per mispredict is the **front-end-to-branch-resolution refi
         ll penalty**. It is a
       7 lower bound on pipeline depth (fetch → execute), not an exact st
     … +56 lines (~2 KB) (ctrl+o to expand)

      5 dependencies and do no work, so the front end is the only limit.
      6 - **Short NOPs:** the limit is an *instruction count* per cycle (

      7   so IPC flattens and bytes/cycle grows with L.
      8 - **Long NOPs:** the limit is *bytes* per cycle (fetch bandwidth)

❯ what are the .S files

● .S files are assembly source files: x86-64 instructions written by hand in GNU
  assembler (AT&T) syntax. The capital S means gcc runs the C preprocessor over
  them first. gcc assembles them and links them with the C files into each
  benchmark binary.

  Why the experiments use them: these benchmarks only work if the CPU runs
  exactly the instructions we chose. In C, the compiler would reorder or delete
  the filler, turn the Q3 branch into a branchless cmov, or collapse a chain of
  adds into a single multiply. Writing the measured part in assembly guarantees
  that what we time is exactly what we wrote. These are also the code snippets
  the report needs.

  The ones in the repo:

  File: common/calib.S
  Contents: Calibration: 1000 dependent add %rdx,%rax, each waiting for the
  previous one, so it runs at exactly 1 add/cycle. That converts rdtsc ticks to
   core cycles. A 3-cycle imul chain checks the result.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: q01_pipelined/bench/kernels.S
  Contents: One dependency chain vs 8 independent chains of add and of imul.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: q03_branch/bench/kernel.S
  Contents: The loop with one data-driven jnz per array element.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: q02/q04/q05/q14 .../kernels.S
  Contents: Generated by Python scripts at build time, so they aren't in git:
  common/chase/gen_chase.py for Q2/Q4/Q5 and q14_fetch_width/bench/gen_nops.py
  for Q14. They contain one function per sweep point (e.g. 101 functions for N
  = 0, 8, …, 800 NOPs).

  Reading the syntax in calib.S:
  asm
  calib_add_chain:        # function name, callable from C as
  calib_add_chain(iters)
      mov  $1, %edx       # rdx = 1          ($ = constant, % = register)
      xor  %eax, %eax     # rax = 0
  1:                      # local label
      .rept 1000          # assembler directive: paste the next line 1000 times
      add  %rdx, %rax     # rax += rdx  (AT&T order: source, destination)
      .endr
      dec  %rdi           # rdi = first C argument (iters); count down
      jnz  1b             # jump back to label 1 ("b" = backward) until zero
      ret

  Other things that appear in the files:
  - .globl makes a function visible to the C code.
  - .p2align 6 aligns code to 64 bytes so where the code lands in memory doesn't
    skew the timing.
  - The .note.GNU-stack line at the bottom tells the linker the stack doesn't
    need to be executable.

  The C side declares each function, e.g. void calib_add_chain(uint64_t iters);
  in common/bench.h, and calls it between two rdtsc reads.

● Trials: 11 for every experiment. run.sh passes -t 11, and each trial times
  every variant once:

  ┌────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Experiment │                     One trial measures                     │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Q1         │ 20 million instructions per variant (20,000 loops ×        │
  │            │ 1,000-instruction block)                                   │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Q2 / Q4 /  │ 20,000 loop iterations (40,000 cache misses) per filler    │
  │ Q5         │ count N; Q4 has 101 N values, Q5 has 81 N values × 3       │
  │            │ filler types                                               │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Q3 / Q15   │ 4 passes over a 1 MiB pattern = ~4.2 million branches per  │
  │            │ pattern                                                    │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Q14        │ ~20 million instructions per NOP length × block size       │
  └────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Each measurement is preceded by an untimed warm-up call, so it starts with
  code, branch predictor and TLB already warm. Reported values are the median,
  with min–max shown as error bars or shaded bands.

  Considerations accounted for:

  Timing correctness
  - rdtsc counts at a fixed rate (TSC), not core cycles. Each run calibrates the
    conversion against a chain of 1-cycle dependent adds.
  - The calibration is checked with a 3-cycle imul chain, which measured 3.00
    every run.
  - Calibration is re-run at the end to measure clock drift; it was ≤1.4%.
  - Before calibrating, the code runs busy for about a second so the CPU reaches
    a steady clock speed.
  - lfence/rdtscp around the timer reads stop work leaking into or out of the
    timed region.

  Noise control
  - Pinned to one core (CPU 2) with taskset.
  - The SMT sibling (CPU 58) is checked for idleness before each run, and its
    load is recorded in the CSV. It was 0–2% busy.
  - Trials are the outer loop, so slow drift spreads across all variants instead
    of biasing one.
  - Medians reject one-off spikes. Q4 showed one: a single-trial spike at N≈250
    that disappeared with more trials.

  Making the experiments measure what they claim
  - Timed code is hand-written assembly, so the compiler can't delete or rewrite
    it.
  - Register-to-register adds only, because recent Intel cores can shortcut
    add-immediate chains.
  - Q2: includes a variant where an instruction uses the first load's result
    before the second load. An in-order core with a non-blocking cache could
    still overlap two back-to-back misses; this variant rules that out.
  - Q2/Q4/Q5 buffers:
    - Two 128 MiB random cyclic chains, far larger than the 52.5 MB L3, so every
      load misses.
    - The random order defeats prefetchers.
    - 4 KB pages, the same as an enclave gets, so native and SGX runs are
      comparable.
  - Q5:
    - Filler avoids zeroing idioms and mov, because rename can eliminate both.
    - Filler chains are short and high-throughput, so they never become the
      bottleneck themselves.
  - Q3:
    - Both branch paths do the same work.
    - The random pattern is 1 MiB long, too long for the predictor to memorize.
    - Learnable patterns with the same 50/50 mix serve as the fair comparison.
  - Q14: block sizes are swept so the µop-cache, legacy-decode and L2-fed
    regimes can be told apart. The L2-fed rate is not reported as fetch width.
  - Results are recorded alongside machine info (machines/artemisia.txt) and the
    exact command line.

  Not yet accounted for
  - No PMU verification yet. That was the plan: timing first, counters later.
  - Only one machine.
  - No step-1 sweep near the Q4/Q5 knees, so they are known only to within ±8.

  SGX: I just checked directly. Right now neither you nor I can run anything in
  an enclave from your account. The SGX hardware itself is on; the blockers are
  access and installation:
  1. Device permission: opening /dev/sgx_enclave fails with PermissionError:
     [Errno 13]. Your account isn't in the sgx group, and without sudo you can't
     add yourself.
  2. Gramine install is incomplete: make q1_bench.manifest fails with No module
     named 'click'. Gramine's own Python package (graminelibos) isn't on the
     machine at all, so Gramine can't build manifests or sign enclaves.

  pip install --user could fix click, but not the missing graminelibos, and the
  device permission still needs an admin. So both items need the course admins
  or TA. Once they're fixed, the harness is ready: ./run.sh sgx 2 builds the
  manifest, signs it and runs. I haven't tested that path, because it can't run
  yet.

❯ I have sudo, how do i add myself to sgx group

● The earlier "no sudo" result came from sudo -n, which fails whenever sudo asks
  for a password. So you probably do have sudo; it just needs your password.

  Your account is an NCSU domain (SSSD) account, not a local one. usermod often
  refuses domain users, so use gpasswd, which edits /etc/group directly:

  sudo gpasswd -a mrthumm2 sgx

  Group changes only apply to new login sessions. Either log out and back in
  (close VS Code's remote session too), or for a quick test in the current
  shell:

  newgrp sgx
  id -nG | tr ' ' '\n' | grep sgx          # should print: sgx
  python3 -c "open('/dev/sgx_enclave','rb'); print('ok')"

  If gpasswd complains that the user doesn't exist, use this instead:

  sudo sed -i 's/^sgx:\(.*\):$/sgx:\1:mrthumm2/' /etc/group   # only if the sgx
  line has no members yet

  Check /etc/group before and after (getent group sgx) to confirm the line looks
  like sgx:x:<gid>:mrthumm2.

  You don't need sgx_prv; that's only for remote attestation.

  Gramine, the second blocker. The missing pip modules are easy:

  sudo python3 -m pip install click tomli tomli_w voluptuous

  But Gramine's own Python package, graminelibos, isn't anywhere on the machine,
  so these alone won't fix gramine-manifest. It's normally installed by
  Gramine's meson install step. Two things to check:

  rpm -qa | grep -i gramine        # installed from a package? then: sudo dnf
  reinstall <pkg>
  ls /usr/local/lib*/python3*/site-packages | grep -i gramine

  If Gramine was built from source, you'll need to reinstall it: sudo meson
  install -C build-release from that source tree, with the same options it was
  built with.

  Once both are fixed, this should succeed end to end:

  cd ~/ECE592_MRES_MRTHUMM2_HW3/q01_pipelined/bench
  ./run.sh sgx 2

  The first run will also create your signing key at
  ~/.config/gramine/enclave-key.pem. Send me the output and I'll check whether
  rdtsc works inside the enclave.

● No, Gramine isn't the only way. The Intel SGX SDK (version 2.23) is also
  installed on Artemisia, at /opt/intel/sgxsdk, with its runtime libraries. The
  options:

  Option: Gramine (current harness)
  How it works: Runs the unmodified Linux binary inside an enclave via a library

  OS.
  Status on Artemisia: Installed but broken: graminelibos is missing.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: Intel SGX SDK
  How it works: You write a small enclave library (.so) containing the benchmark

  kernels. A normal host program calls into it through ECALLs defined in an
  .edl file.
  Status on Artemisia: Installed (SDK and runtime 2.23).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: Open Enclave, Occlum, SCONE
  How it works: Other SDKs and library OSes.
  Status on Artemisia: Not installed.

  All of these need the sgx group fix. Every SGX runtime opens /dev/sgx_enclave,
  so gpasswd -a mrthumm2 sgx is required either way. Only the Gramine Python
  problem is specific to Gramine.

  Comparing the two realistic options:
  - Gramine: no code changes; ./run.sh sgx 2 already works once the install is
    fixed. The whole program runs inside the enclave, including setup and
    output. One cost: Gramine itself sits between your code and the CPU, which
    is why gramine-direct exists as a control.
  - Intel SDK:
    - No dependency on the broken Gramine install.
    - Only the timed kernels run in the enclave; setup and printing stay
      outside. That's arguably a cleaner experiment.
    - It needs more code: an EDL file, an enclave config, a signing step, and an
      enclave-side wrapper per experiment.
    - Enclave code can't use normal libc (printf, malloc work differently), so
      the kernels plus a thin wrapper go inside and everything else stays in the
      host.
    - It's also the natural fit for the "time the whole enclave call from
      outside" fallback if rdtsc is blocked in the enclave, since the host can
      time each ECALL.

  My recommendation: do the sgx group fix first, then try fixing Gramine. If rpm
  -qa | grep gramine shows nothing (it showed nothing just now), Gramine was
  built from source and fixing it may mean a rebuild. Then the SDK is likely the
  faster path. I can add an SDK-based enclave mode to the harness. The .S
  kernels can be linked into the enclave unchanged, so each experiment needs
  only an EDL file and a small wrapper.

  Want me to start on the SDK path now, so you aren't waiting on the Gramine
  fix?

❯ how to normally add sudo to sgx without gpasswd

● The standard way is usermod:

  sudo usermod -aG sgx mrthumm2

  - -G sgx sets the supplementary group.
  - -a appends it. Without -a, usermod -G replaces all your supplementary groups
    with just sgx. On this account that would drop the ~150 NCSU groups (or
    fail on the domain ones). Always include -a.

  Caveat for your account: mrthumm2 comes from the NCSU domain (via SSSD), not
  from /etc/passwd. usermod only manages local users, so it may fail with:

  usermod: user 'mrthumm2' does not exist in /etc/passwd

  If that happens, here are the other ways, all of which just add your name to
  the sgx line in /etc/group:

  # Edit /etc/group safely (locks the file, checks syntax on save)
  sudo vigr
  #   find:    sgx:x:<gid>:
  #   change:  sgx:x:<gid>:mrthumm2
  # then also run: sudo vigr -s   (the shadow group file) and make the same edit
  if an sgx line exists

  Then verify:

  getent group sgx            # should list mrthumm2

  Then log out and back in, or run newgrp sgx in the current shell. Group
  membership is only read at login, so id won't show sgx until you start a new
  session. VS Code Remote keeps its server process alive, so you may need to
  fully reconnect, or kill the server with pkill -u mrthumm2 -f vscode-server,
  before the integrated terminal picks it up.

         +x` group

      14 +## What it checks                                              
      15 +1. `rdtsc` inside the enclave: does it fault? Is the value cons
         +istent with host reads?                                        
      16 +2. Back-to-back `rdtsc` deltas inside the enclave, compared wit
         +h native. A trapped and                                        
      17 +   emulated `rdtsc` would show deltas of thousands of ticks.   
      18 +3. The same 2·10⁷-cycle dependent add chain timed three ways: n
         +ative, `rdtsc` inside the                                      
      19 +   enclave, and the host timing the whole ECALL.

      21 +

         +0-0551`). It inserts                                           
      23 +`lfence` after loads, which would distort the out-of-order expe
         +riments.

      27 +rdtsc in enclave: OK, value within host bracket: yes           
      28 +back-to-back rdtsc delta in enclave: min 48, median 54 ticks

      29 +cycles per TSC tick:  native 1.34312 | rdtsc inside enclave 1.3
         +3518 | host times ECALL 1.33550                                
      30 +empty ECALL round trip: 11833 ticks = 15902 cycles

         +Sapphire Rapids. There is                                      
      33 +  no fault and no emulation; resolution matches native. So the 
         +same in-process timing                                         
      34 +  and calibration can be used in the enclave.

         +tive.

         +m the host also agrees,

         +1%. Any measurement                                            
      38 +  timed from outside must be ≫ 16k cycles.

● The timers work inside the enclave. I built a small test enclave with the
  Intel SGX SDK, ran it in hardware debug mode on CPU 2, and compared it with
  native:

  ┌───────────────────────┬───────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Check         │    Native     │       Inside SGX enclave        │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │                       │               │ yes: no fault, and the value    │
  │ rdtsc executes        │ yes           │ falls between host reads taken  │
  │                       │               │ before and after                │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Back-to-back rdtsc    │ min 40 /      │                                 │
  │ gap                   │ median 52     │ min 48 / median 54 ticks        │
  │                       │ ticks         │                                 │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Cycles per TSC tick   │               │ 1.33518 timed inside; 1.33550   │
  │ (2·10⁷-add chain)     │ 1.34312       │ with the host timing the whole  │
  │                       │               │ call                            │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Empty enclave call    │ –             │ ~11.8k ticks ≈ 15.9k cycles     │
  │ (ECALL) round trip    │               │                                 │
  └───────────────────────┴───────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  - rdtsc is native in the enclave. If it were trapped and emulated, the
    back-to-back gap would be thousands of ticks, not ~50. So the benchmarks can
    keep timing themselves from inside the enclave, using the same calibration.
  - Calibration matches native within 0.6%.
  - Timing from outside also works, if the work is long. Each enclave call costs
    ~16k cycles, so anything timed from the host must take far longer than
    that. The 20-million-cycle chain hides it below 0.1%.
  - I left the SDK's LVI mitigation off on purpose. It inserts lfence after
    loads, which would distort the out-of-order experiments. That's worth a
    sentence in the report.

  The test code and results are in common/sgx/timer_probe/, and its README
  records the numbers. Its signing key was created at
  ~/.config/sgx/enclave_private.pem, outside the repo. I also updated the
  top-level README: SGX access is resolved, the timer results are noted, and
  Gramine is still listed as broken.

  My own shell still runs under the old login, so I ran the test through sg sgx.
  In your relaunched terminal, ./probe_app should work directly.

  The next step is an SGX mode for the benchmarks. Each experiment's assembly
  kernels would go inside an SDK enclave, with the host program doing setup and
  output. Then ./run.sh sgx 2 works for all seven experiments without waiting on
  Gramine. Should I build that?

❯ yes lets build it without gramine

● Building an SDK-based SGX backend for all seven experiments. The design:
  - Each experiment's timed part (kernels, data setup, the timed call) moves
    into a small "kernel-side" file. That file compiles both into the native
    binary and into an enclave.
  - The host driver talks to it through a thin backend: a direct call for
    native, an ECALL for SGX.
  - Timing happens inside the enclave, and test data (pointer chains, branch
    patterns) lives in enclave memory, so we measure EPC behaviour.

  First the shared pieces:

● Bash(cd /home/mrthumm2/ECE592_MRES_MRTHUMM2_HW3/common && cat bench.h | sed -n
      1,20p && grep -n
      "xorshift64\|ticks_of\|bench_calibrate\|bench_recheck\|measure_cpt" b…)

      * Timing is TSC-based (no PMU). rdtsc ticks at a fixed frequency, so

  can use it:

        1 +/* Pointer-chase timed part shared by Q2 / Q4 / Q5 (native + e
          +nclave).

        3 + * kt_setup(NULL, 0, mb_per_chain, seed, thp) builds two indep
          +endent random                                                 
        4 + * cyclic pointer chains, each over its own buffer much larger
          + than the LLC,

          + come from the

      66  ## Enclave notes
      67  Buffers use 4 KB pages (default; `-H` off) so native and SGX ru
          ns are comparable. Expect
      68  a larger absolute miss cost in SGX (memory encryption), but the
           same *ratios*.
      69 -Two 128 MiB chains need `ENCLAVE_SIZE` ≥ 512M (default in `comm
         -on/common.mk`).                                                
      69 +In SGX mode the chains are built in the enclave heap (`ENCLAVE_
         +HEAP` = 288 MiB in `bench/Makefile`), i.e. in encrypted EPC mem
         +ory.

       1 # SGX backend (Intel SGX SDK, no Gramine)
       2
       3 Every experiment can run its timed part inside an SGX enclave wi
         th
       4 `./run.sh sgx <cpu>`. The host driver (CLI, data generation, CSV
          output) is the same
       5 code in both modes. Only the backend linked into it changes.
       6

● All seven experiments now run inside an SGX enclave using the Intel SGX SDK,
  with no Gramine involved. ./run.sh sgx 2 works for every experiment, and I ran
  the full suite in both modes (11 trials each, CPU 2). Each plot overlays
  artemisia_native against artemisia_sgx.

  How it works:
  - Each experiment's timed part (kernels, rdtsc timing, test data) is in one
    file that compiles into both builds: q1_kt.c, common/chase/chase_kt.c,
    q3_kt.c and q14_kt.c.
  - The driver code is shared, so the two modes produce identical CSV output.
  - In SGX mode the timed part runs inside the enclave. The pointer chains are
    built in enclave memory and the branch patterns are copied in, so Q2/Q4/Q5
    misses really hit encrypted enclave memory (EPC).
  - Calibration also runs inside the enclave.
  - An ECALL costs about 16k cycles, but none happens inside a timed region.
  - make sgx builds the host program and the signed enclave. run.sh sgx first
    checks that your session can open /dev/sgx_enclave and tells you what to do
    if it can't.
  - common/sgx/README.md describes the design and lists the configuration to
    report.

  Native vs. enclave:

  ┌─────┬───────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │  Q  │                Native                 │            SGX            │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 1   │ Independent adds 4.73 instr/cycle;    │ Identical                 │
  │     │ imul 1.00 vs 0.333                    │                           │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 2   │ 1 miss = 361 cycles; independent pair │ 415 cycles (+15%); pair   │
  │     │  1.01×                                │ 1.04×; same conclusion    │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 3   │ Random 14.1 vs 3.0–3.6 cycles/branch  │ Identical                 │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │     │                                       │ Same knee, higher         │
  │ 4   │ Knee at N = 496→504                   │ plateaus (436 vs 374      │
  │     │                                       │ cycles)                   │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 5   │ int 208→216, mix 424→432, ymm 240→248 │ int and mix identical;    │
  │     │                                       │ ymm 256→264               │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 14  │ 6 instr/cycle, ≥29 B/cycle legacy     │ Identical                 │
  │     │ fetch, 12.8 B/cycle when fed from L2  │                           │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 15  │ 22.3 cycles per mispredict            │ 22.2                      │
  └─────┴───────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  The enclave makes memory accesses 15% slower but doesn't change any core
  structure (pipeline, ROB, register files, predictor, front end). That's the
  comparison the report needs.

  Not settled yet:
  - Q5 ymm knee: the shift isn't proven. Natively the ymm transition is ragged
    across N = 240–272, so it overlaps the enclave's clean step. A sweep at
    every N from 232 to 280 would settle it.
  - All-taken / all-not-taken branch patterns: these flip between about 2.0 and
    3.2 cycles from trial to trial in both modes, more often in the enclave. It
    doesn't affect any conclusion, but I don't know the cause.
  - Q4 outliers: the min–max band shows one or two slow trials out of 11. That
    matches another user's cpptools process using about 8 cores on Artemisia.
    The medians aren't affected. Native Q1 and Q3 had a busy SMT sibling the
    first time, so I re-ran them with it idle.

  Changes to existing code:
  - Gramine removed: the template and rules are gone, and the run modes are now
    native|sgx.
  - Calibration signature: bench_calibrate() and bench_recheck() take a timer
    argument, so calibration can run inside the enclave. I updated the timer
    probe to match.
  - Docs: the top-level and per-experiment READMEs now include SGX run commands
    and results, with the Q4 plateau numbers checked against the data.

  Nothing is committed yet. Next is the second machine. The same run.sh native
  works there; it can only run sgx too if that machine has SGX.

● That settles the main question. Summary per experiment:

  Short answer: The Artemisia native-vs-SGX data is consistent and mostly
  supportable: typical trial-to-trial spread is under 1.5%, and every main
  conclusion holds in every trial. There are two gaps:
  1. The second machine is missing for every question. Track B requires each
     selected exercise on two course machines, side by side. This is the largest
     gap, and it's due in two days.
  2. Q15's penalty number and Q5's register-file interpretation can't be
     supported yet.

  Per experiment:

  Q: 1 Pipelined
  Data on Artemisia: 11 trials × 2 modes, IQR ≤ 0.7%
  Makes sense?: Yes. 4.73 adds/cycle is close to the published 5 ALUs, and imul
  runs at exactly 1/cycle vs its 3-cycle latency
  Supportable now?: Yes
  Work needed: Second machine only. Note that add_dep = 1.0 is true by
  construction (it is the calibration); the independent check is imul = 3.00
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Q: 2 OoO
  Data on Artemisia: IQR ≤ 1.8%
  Makes sense?: Yes. Serial 2.00×, independent 1.01×, with a consumer 1.04×.
  Native miss ≈ 361 cycles ≈ 135 ns, which includes a page walk per load (4 KB
  pages over 128 MiB)
  Supportable now?: Yes
  Work needed: Second machine only
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Q: 3 Branch pred.
  Data on Artemisia: Random/alt/period patterns are stable
  Makes sense?: Yes. 14.1 vs 3.0 cycles is unambiguous
  Supportable now?: Yes
  Work needed: Second machine. taken/nottaken flip between 2.0 and 3.2 cycles
  per
  trial (IQR up to 52% in SGX). That's 2 taken branches per loop iteration, a
  front-end throughput effect unrelated to prediction; explain it, or drop
  those two from the headline
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Q: 4 ROB
  Data on Artemisia: Knee identical in every trial in both modes: 488 full
  overlap, 496 partial, 504 serialized
  Makes sense?: Yes. ROB ≈ 498–506 vs the published 512
  Supportable now?: Mostly
  Work needed: Run the existing zoom.sh (step 1, N = 470–530) in both modes to
  pin the knee and account for the ~10-entry gap to 512. Second machine
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Q: 5 PRF
  Data on Artemisia: int knee 208→216 in every trial, both modes; mix ≈ 2× int
  Makes sense?: The class-separation result is solid
  Supportable now?: Partially. "Separate int/vector files" holds; "PRF = X"
  doesn't yet. Free int ≈ 212 vs the published 280, and the ~70 gap is
  unexplained
  Work needed: Add a flags-free filler (e.g. lea) to test the gap. Also see the
  ymm note below
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Q: 14 Fetch
  Data on Artemisia: IQR ~0.1% except at the µop-cache boundary
  Makes sense?: Yes. 6.0 instr/cycle is Golden Cove's 6-wide rename; the 12.8
  B/cycle flat line starts exactly at a 32 KiB footprint (the L1i size)
  Supportable now?: Yes, as bounds: 6-wide, legacy fetch ≥ 29 B/cycle
  Work needed: Optional: K = 4608 to hit the 32 B/cycle ceiling. Second machine
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Q: 15 Depth
  Data on Artemisia: Stable
  Makes sense?: No, not fully. The implied penalty drifts from 43 to 22 cycles
  as
  F rises, so the "mispredicts = min(F, 1−F)" model is wrong somewhere
  Supportable now?: Only the F = 0.5 value (22 cycles)
  Work needed: Needs PMU: perf stat -e branch-misses per F (paranoid = 2 allows
  it). Also, the native-vs-SGX difference at low F (42.8 vs 37.3) comes
  entirely from the bimodal nottaken baseline. Use alt/period:16 as the
  baseline

  Two things I found while checking:

  1. External interference comes in episodes. In Q4 and Q5, whole trials ran ~2×
     slow across many consecutive N values: native trials 7–8 and SGX trial 9.
     That's time-correlated, most likely memory contention from other users'
     jobs on Artemisia. It shows up only in the min–max bands, never in the
     medians or the knees. For the report: say so, plot medians, and ideally
     repeat the suite once at a quieter time to show reproducibility.
  2. The Q5 ymm native-vs-SGX difference holds up across trials, and it's more
     interesting than noise. Natively, the ymm knee is at about 240–248 in 9 of
     11 trials and about 256–264 in 2. In SGX it's at 256–264 in all 11 trials.
     So there seem to be two states, and the enclave is always in the later one.
     My hypothesis is that the vector "upper state" (AVX-512 / XSAVE in-use
     tracking) differs inside the enclave; it's untested. You can report it as
     an observed enclave difference with the trial evidence. A test is possible
     (e.g. dirty zmm state before running natively), but it's optional given the
     deadline.

  Priorities for the remaining two days:
  1. Second machine: run all 7 experiments natively (plus SGX if it has it).
     That's about 2 minutes per mode, and it's required for every question.
  2. Q4 zoom in both modes, because it's worth 30 points and the script already
     exists.
  3. Q15 branch-misses: this turns 22 cycles into a defensible number. It's also
     your PMU verification step; adding cycles,instructions for Q1 costs almost
     nothing extra.
  4. Q5 lea filler, plus step-1 sweeps around the int and ymm knees.
  5. One full repeat run on Artemisia to show reproducibility.
  6. AI_LOG.md, which is still missing and required.

  I can do 2–4 now on Artemisia while you set up the second machine. Want me to?

  ⎿  Created common/pmu_lib.sh (+15 -0)
       1 +# Sourced by the pmu.sh scripts. PMU counters are used only to 
         +VERIFY the                                                     
       2 +# TSC-based results (see top-level README, "PMU policy"); nativ
         +e mode only,                                                   
       3 +# since counters are not visible inside SGX enclaves.          
       4 +#                                                              
       5 +#   pmu_count CPU EVENTS CMD...   -> prints "ev1=count ev2=coun
         +t ..." (user-space counts)                                     
       6 +#                                                              
       7 +# Callers use a difference method: run the same command twice w
         +ith more or                                                    
       8 +# less timed work and subtract, so calibration, setup and warm-
         +up cancel out.                                                 
       9 +                                                               
      10 +pmu_count() {                                                  
      11 +    local cpu=$1 events=$2                                     
      12 +    shift 2                                                    
      13 +    perf stat -x, -e "$events" -o /dev/stderr taskset -c "$cpu"
         + "$@" 2>&1 >/dev/null \                                        
      14 +        | awk -F, '$1 ~ /^[0-9]+$/ { split($3, e, ":"); printf 
         +"%s=%s ", e[1], $1 } END { print "" }'                         
      15 +}                                                              
  ⎿  Created q15_pipeline_depth/bench/pmu.sh (+36 -0)
       1 +#!/bin/bash                                                    
       2 +# PMU verification for Q3/Q15 (native only): measured branch-mi
         +ss rate and                                                    
       3 +# PMU cycles per data branch for each pattern, by the differenc
         +e method:                                                      
       4 +#   run with -n 4 and -n 8 timed passes; delta = TRIALS * 4 * L
         +EN data branches.                                              
       5 +# -> ../results/<machine>/pmu/native.csv                       
       6 +# usage: ./pmu.sh [cpu]                                        
       7 +set -euo pipefail                                              
       8 +cd "$(dirname "$0")"                                           
       9 +source ../../common/run_lib.sh                                 
      10 +source ../../common/pmu_lib.sh                                 
      11 +                                                               
      12 +CPU=${1:-2}                                                    
      13 +TRIALS=3                                                       
      14 +LEN=$((1 << 20))                                               
      15 +EVENTS=branches:u,branch-misses:u,cycles:u,instructions:u      
      16 +PATTERNS="nottaken taken alt period:16 random"                 
      17 +for f in 0.05 0.10 0.15 0.20 0.25 0.30 0.35 0.40 0.45 0.50; do 
         +PATTERNS+=" bias:$f"; done                                     
      18 +                                                               
      19 +make -s                                                        
      20 +out="../results/$(machine_name)/pmu/native.csv"                
      21 +mkdir -p "$(dirname "$out")"                                   
      22 +{                                                              
      23 +    echo "# host $(machine_name) mode native cpu $CPU date $(da
         +te -Is) method=diff(-n 8 minus -n 4) trials=$TRIALS len=$LEN"  
      24 +    echo "pattern,data_branches,d_branches,d_branch_misses,d_cy
         +cles,d_instructions,miss_rate,pmu_cyc_per_branch"              
      25 +    for p in $PATTERNS; do                                     
      26 +        declare -A a=() b=()                                   
      27 +        for kv in $(pmu_count "$CPU" "$EVENTS" ./q15_bench -t $
         +TRIALS -n 4 -p "$p"); do a[${kv%%=*}]=${kv#*=}; done           
      28 +        for kv in $(pmu_count "$CPU" "$EVENTS" ./q15_bench -t $
         +TRIALS -n 8 -p "$p"); do b[${kv%%=*}]=${kv#*=}; done           
      29 +        n=$(( TRIALS * 4 * LEN ))                              
      30 +        db=$(( b[branches] - a[branches] )); dm=$(( b[branch-mi
         +sses] - a[branch-misses] ))                                    
      31 +        dc=$(( b[cycles] - a[cycles] )); di=$(( b[instructions]
         + - a[instructions] ))                                          
      32 +        echo "$p,$n,$db,$dm,$dc,$di,$(awk -v m=$dm -v n=$n 'BEG
         +IN{printf "%.5f", m/n}'),$(awk -v c=$dc -v n=$n 'BEGIN{printf "
         +%.4f", c/n}')"                                                 
      33 +        echo "  $p done" >&2                                   
      34 +    done                                                       
      35 +} > "$out"                                                     
      36 +echo "wrote $out" >&2

      76 +- **The drift was the model, not the machine.** On biased noise
         + the predictor mispredicts                                     
      77 +  much more than the minority fraction: ~1.7× F at small F. It 
         +presumably keeps finding                                       
      78 +  spurious patterns in the global history and following them. W
         +ith the measured miss rate                                     
      79 +  the penalty is **flat: 22.4–23.6 cycles, median 22.8**.      
      80 +- Result: **≈ 23 cycles lost per mispredict.** This is a lower 
         +bound on the fetch→execute                                     
      81 +  depth: it measures the refill from the front end to branch re
         +solution, minus any overlap.                                   
      82 +  For context, published Golden Cove figures are ~17 cycles min
         +imum, more when the refill                                     
      83 +  misses the µop cache.                                        
      84 +- Here PMU cycles per branch come from the same counters, so th
         +e timing and miss counts are                                   
      85 +  self-consistent. They agree with the TSC timing within a few 
         +percent for most patterns                                      
      86 +  (`random` 14.28 vs 14.14; `nottaken` 2.05 vs 2.00). `alt` rea
         +ds 2.66 (PMU, steady state)                                    
      87 +  vs 3.00 (TSC); this changes the baseline by < 0.4 cycles and 
         +the penalty by < 1 cycle.                                      
      88 +- Counters are not visible inside the enclave, so this check is
         + native only. The SGX timing                                   
      89 +  (22.2 cycles at F = 0.5) matches native, so the same miss rat
         +es apply.

● All three tasks are done on Artemisia, and each turned an open question into a
  supported result. Nothing is committed yet. Everything is written up in the
  per-question READMEs and in the top-level README's results table.

  Q4, ROB (step-1 sweep, 21 trials, native and SGX): the second miss is fully
  serialized from N = 498 in both modes, after a short partial ramp over
  493–497. In 18 of 21 native trials and all 21 SGX trials, the per-trial jump
  lands at 497–498. That puts the ROB at about 500 entries, 2.3% below the
  published 512.

  Q15, mispredict penalty (PMU check): counting with branch-misses showed the
  predictor mispredicts much more than I assumed on biased random patterns. At
  5% taken it mispredicts 8.9% of the time, about 1.7× the minority fraction.
  That wrong assumption caused the penalty drift. With the measured miss rates,
  the penalty is flat at 22.4–23.6 cycles (median 22.8) at every taken rate, so
  about 23 cycles per mispredict is now defensible. The same counters confirm
  Q3: zero mispredicts on the learnable patterns and 0.4997 on random.

  Q1 (PMU check): counted IPC matches the timing-based IPC (1.001, 4.72, 0.331,
  1.02). This also independently confirms the TSC-to-cycle calibration, which
  had been circular because the dependent add chain is 1.0 by definition.

  Q5, register files (step-1 sweeps plus 5 control fillers, both modes):
  - About 210 free integer registers. Flags are ruled out: register-only writers
    with no flags (shlx, lea r,r) hit the same limit, 207–215, as flag-writing
    add and andn.
  - lea reg+imm allocates no physical register at all. With that filler the
    window runs to the ROB limit, so Golden Cove evidently handles it at rename.
  - Integer and vector are separate files: the alternating int/ymm knee (~428)
    is about twice the int knee.
  - The native-vs-SGX vector difference is explained (244 free natively vs 262
    in the enclave). glibc's AVX-512 string routines leave ymm16–31 in use
    natively, and those registers hold physical vector registers; the enclave's
    library never touches them. When the test writes ymm16–31 first, the enclave
    also drops to 244, and nothing changes natively. So it's architectural
    state, not the enclave changing the hardware. It's a good native-vs-enclave
    discussion point for the report.

  The published integer register file is 280 entries, against about 210 measured
  free. Part of the gap is committed architectural registers, possibly the idle
  SMT sibling's too. The report should give 210 as the measured free count and
  the total as a lower bound.

  Other changes:
  - q1_bench has a new -v option to run a single variant.
  - New scripts: pmu.sh for Q1 and Q15, and q05_prf/bench/ymm_state.sh.
  - common/pmu_verify.sh now calls the new pmu.sh scripts for Q1/Q3/Q15. It
    previously counted whole processes, including calibration and setup.
  - The knee detector in common/knee.py was flagging slow upward drift as a
    knee. I tightened its threshold; Q4's result is unchanged.

  Still to do: the second machine, which is required for every question and due
  in two days. Artemisia's data is now in good shape, so all of the remaining
  risk is there. After that come the report PDF, AI_LOG.md and the ZIP.

❯ Okay great, so there are seperate modes to run PMU check and the main
  expriment right? Also after using PMU verificaiton, did you edit Q5 original
  benches to fix the misconception from before?

● Separate modes: yes. The main experiments never touch the PMU, and the PMU
  checks run separately afterwards, as your PMU policy says:

  Script: bench/run.sh <mode> <cpu>
  What it does: Main experiment, timed with rdtsc only
  Modes: native, sgx
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Script: q04_rob/bench/zoom.sh, q05_prf/bench/zoom.sh
  What it does: Same timing method, step-1 sweep around the knees
  Modes: native, sgx
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Script: q05_prf/bench/ymm_state.sh
  What it does: Same timing method, ymm vs ymm-after-writing-ymm16–31
  Modes: native, sgx
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Script: q01_pipelined/bench/pmu.sh, q15_pipeline_depth/bench/pmu.sh
  What it does: PMU verification with perf stat counters
  Modes: native only (counters aren't visible inside an enclave)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Script: common/pmu_verify.sh
  What it does: Runs both pmu.sh scripts, plus a whole-process Q14 count
  Modes: native only

  Results stay separate too: timing in results/<machine>/<mode>.csv (plus zoom/
  and ymm_state/), and counters in results/<machine>/pmu/.

  Q5: the PMU wasn't involved in Q5 at all. It still has no PMU check, because
  it would need microarchitecture-specific stall events that I haven't set up.
  Its open question was settled with timing experiments, by adding new control
  fillers to the same benchmark.

  I didn't edit the original Q5 benchmark, because nothing in it was wrong:
  - The original int filler (add r,r) was a valid register-file test. The
    controls showed that every integer writer that allocates a register gives
    the same knee (207–215), with or without flags. So the old data and its ~210
    knee stand.
  - The misconception was only in the interpretation. The README had raised
    flags as a possible cause; it now says flags are ruled out.
  - One real hazard turned up: lea reg+imm would have been a bad filler, since
    it doesn't allocate a register. The original bench never used it, and the
    README now warns against it.
  - The generator is unchanged for the old fillers. It only gained the control
    kinds and the ymm_hi variant, so the original sweeps produce the same
    kernels as before.

  Q15 is different: its original analysis did have the misconception, and I
  haven't fixed the original plot. q15_pipeline_depth/plots/plot.py still
  divides by the assumed miss rate min(F, 1−F), so it still shows the drifting
  42→22 penalty. The corrected numbers exist only in the new plot_pmu.py. The
  Q15 README's "Interpreting results" section also still has a stale line saying
  PMU verification is needed.

  My suggestion: have plot.py use the measured miss rates from
  results/<machine>/pmu/native.csv when that file exists, and keep the assumed
  rate as a labeled fallback. That fallback is still needed for machines without
  perf access, and for the SGX series, which uses the native rates. Then fix
  the stale README line. Should I make that change?
❯ So are you saying without PMUs, we can't fix our initial measurement
  experiment of Q15, or is there anything that needs to change that we can
  change to make it more accurate?

● No, we don't need the PMU to get Q15 right. The timing data was fine; my
  analysis leaned on the wrong data points. Here's why, then I'll fix the
  analysis.

  Which measurements have a known miss rate without the PMU:
  - Fair coin (F = 0.5, random) has an exact miss rate of 0.5. The outcomes are
    independent of everything the predictor can see, so no predictor can do
    better or worse than 50% on average. No counter is needed.
  - Biased coins (F < 0.5) only have a lower bound. The best possible predictor
    mispredicts min(F, 1−F); a real one, like ours, can only do worse. So
    dividing by min(F, 1−F) gives an upper bound on the penalty, never the
    actual value. That's exactly the drift we saw: 42 → 22, tightening toward
    the exact point at F = 0.5.
  - The baseline should be alt / period:16, not nottaken. They have the same
    50/50 path mix as random and are perfectly predicted. You can tell that from
    timing alone, because they all sit on the same minimum cost. nottaken is
    the bimodal 2.0/3.2-cycle pattern, which is what skewed the SGX low-F
    numbers.

  So the PMU-free method is: penalty = (t_random − t_alt) / 0.5, with the
  biased-F points shown as upper bounds. Let me check the numbers and test the
  reverse: with the penalty known, can timing alone predict the miss rate at
  each F, and does that match what the PMU counted?

      17 +## Method (timing only)                                        
      18 +**Anchor: a fair coin.** For i.i.d. 50/50 outcomes (`bias:0.50`
         +), *every* predictor                                           
      19 +mispredicts exactly half the time in expectation. The outcome i
         +s independent of everything                                    
      20 +the predictor can observe, so no history, table or algorithm he
         +lps or hurts. With `alt` as

      22 +```                                                            
      23 +P = (t(bias:0.50) − t_alt) / 0.5

      29 +**Biased coins (F < 0.5) only bound the miss rate from below.**
         + The best possible predictor                                   
      30 +mispredicts min(F, 1−F), and a real one can only do worse. So  
      31 +`(t(F) − base(F)) / min(F, 1−F)` is an **upper bound** on P, ti
         +ghtest at F = 0.5.                                             
      32 +Given P, the timing *implies* the actual miss rate at each F:  
      33  ```
      26 -base(F)    = t_nottaken + 2·F·(t_alt − t_nottaken)   # perfectl
         -y predicted cost at this path mix                              
      27 -penalty(F) = (t(F) − base(F)) / F                              
      34 +base(F) = t_one + 2·F·(t_alt − t_one)      # perfectly predicte
         +d cost at path mix F

         +an of taken / nottaken                                         
      36  ```
      29 -The F = 0.5 point is the textbook "(random − predictable) / mis

● No, Q15 doesn't need the PMU. Timing alone gives the right answer; my first
  analysis leaned on the wrong data points. I've fixed the analysis. The
  measurements themselves didn't need to change.

  Why timing alone is enough:
  - A 50/50 random pattern has a known miss rate. Every branch predictor
    mispredicts a fair coin exactly half the time on average, because the
    outcome is independent of anything it can see. So penalty = (t_random −
    t_alt) / 0.5 is exact.
  - Biased patterns only give an upper bound. No predictor can mispredict less
    than min(F, 1−F), and a real one does worse. Dividing by that undercounts
    mispredicts, so the result overestimates the penalty. My old 42→22-cycle
    "drift" was a series of upper bounds tightening toward F = 0.5.
  - The right baseline is alt (1010…). It has the same 50/50 mix and is
    perfectly predicted. The old analysis also used nottaken, which flips
    between 2.0 and 3.2 cycles from trial to trial.

  Results, timing only:

  ┌────────┬─────────────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │        │  Penalty (alt   │ With period:16   │   PMU, for comparison    │
  │        │    baseline)    │     baseline     │                          │
  ├────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Native │ 22.3 cycles     │ 22.5             │ 22.8 (flat across all F) │
  ├────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ SGX    │ 22.2 cycles     │ 22.6             │ counters not available   │
  │        │                 │                  │ in the enclave           │
  └────────┴─────────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  Timing also reproduces the PMU's miss rates. With the penalty fixed from the
  fair coin, timing predicts the miss rate at every taken probability within
  0.007 of what the PMU counted, e.g. 0.096 vs 0.089 at F = 0.05 and 0.339 vs
  0.339 at F = 0.25. That matters for the enclave, where the counters are
  hidden: the SGX timing gives the same miss rates as native, so the predictor
  behaves the same inside the enclave. In the report, the PMU becomes what your
  plan intended, a verification step rather than a requirement.

  Changed:
  - plots/plot.py: rewritten to use this method.
    - Left panel: cost per branch, with the penalty in the legend.
    - Right panel: miss rate implied by timing, both modes, with the
      best-possible-predictor line.
    - PMU overlay: the measured miss rates are drawn as ✕ marks, only when
      results/<machine>/pmu/native.csv exists. So the plot works on a machine
      without perf.
  - Q15 README: new method section and results tables. The stale "needs PMU
    verification" line is gone, and there's a short note on why the first
    analysis drifted, worth a sentence in the report.
  - Top-level README: the Q15 results row is updated.

  Caveat: the implied miss rates at small F depend slightly on the baseline.
  Because taken/nottaken flip between two speeds, the baseline is uncertain by a
  few tenths of a cycle. That's why SGX reads 0.084 at F = 0.05 against
  native's 0.096. The headline penalty uses only alt and the fair coin, so it
  isn't affected.

❯ okay great so we can take this current repo and scripts and run on the second
  machine, and be able to collect data for all of our questions?

● Almost. The scripts are portable in the obvious ways: results go to
  results/<hostname>/ automatically, the native build needs only gcc, make and
  python3, SGX mode is skipped cleanly on a machine without it, and pmu.sh is
  optional. But checking through them, I found three things that would break or
  silently give bad data on a different CPU:

  1. Q5 would crash on any CPU without AVX-512. The ymm_hi filler I just added
     uses ymm16–31, which only exist with AVX-512. It's built into the default
     set, so run.sh would hit an illegal instruction on e.g. a Skylake desktop,
     Alder Lake client or AMD Zen 2/3.
  2. The step-1 zoom windows are hard-coded for Sapphire Rapids. Q4 zooms N =
     470–530 (ROB ≈ 500) and Q5 zooms 196–280 / 404–460. On a Skylake (ROB 224)
     or Zen 3 (ROB 256), the knee would fall outside those windows and zoom.sh
     would quietly measure flat lines.
  3. Nothing checks the other machine's prerequisites:
     - CPU features: AVX2 for the ymm fillers, BMI1/BMI2 for the andn/shlx
       controls.
     - LLC size: the 128 MiB chase buffers must exceed the last-level cache.
       Some AMD EPYC parts have 256 MiB+ of L3.
     - Allowed CPUs for taskset, and the python modules.

  I'll fix all three now. First, the zoom windows: instead of hard-coding them,
  zoom.sh will derive them from that machine's own coarse sweep.

● Yes. After this round of fixes, the repo will collect data for all seven
  questions on the second machine with two commands:

  common/preflight.sh 2     # check the machine first
  common/run_all.sh 2       # run everything (~10 min per mode), then plot

  run_all.sh runs, in order:
  1. All seven experiments.
  2. The Q4/Q5 step-1 zooms.
  3. The Q5 vector-state test.
  4. The PMU checks, if perf works there.
  5. All plots.

  It adds the SGX mode automatically if that machine has a usable
  /dev/sgx_enclave. Results land in q*/results/<hostname>/, and the plots in
  plots/compare/ overlay both machines. I tested that with a simulated second
  machine with no PMU and no AVX-512 data; all plot scripts worked, and I
  removed the test copy afterwards.

  What I fixed so it works on a different CPU:
  - Q5 no longer crashes on CPUs without AVX-512. The ymm16–31 test is only
    built where the CPU has AVX-512VL, and the andn/shlx controls only where it
    has BMI1/BMI2. ymm_state.sh skips with a message otherwise. I checked this
    by building for a simulated CPU without those features.
  - Zoom windows follow each machine's own knees. They used to be hard-coded for
    Sapphire Rapids, which would have missed a Skylake or Zen knee entirely. On
    Artemisia the derived windows match the ones we chose by hand (472–528 and
    184–272 / 400–456). You can override them with ZOOM=lo:hi ./zoom.sh ….
  - The kernels rebuild themselves when the window changes, using a small stamp
    file.
  - Preflight check (common/preflight.sh) covers:
    - tools, and numpy/matplotlib;
    - required CPU features (AVX2 is mandatory for Q2/Q4/Q5);
    - whether you can pin to the chosen CPU, and which core is its SMT sibling;
    - last-level cache size vs. the 128 MiB pointer chains, warning on
      large-cache parts like some AMD EPYCs;
    - SGX device and SDK, and perf access.

    It passes cleanly on Artemisia.

  What you need to do:
  1. Commit and push first. You've already staged some of the files, but these
     new ones aren't tracked yet: common/preflight.sh, common/run_all.sh and
     common/zoom_window.py. Several modified files aren't staged either
     (common/common.mk, common/run_lib.sh, common/plot_all.sh, both zoom
     scripts, the Q4/Q5 Makefiles, .gitignore). The second machine only gets
     what's in git.
  2. On the second machine, pick a CPU you're allowed to use (taskset -cp $$
     lists them) whose SMT sibling is idle. Pass it to both scripts.
  3. Add the second machine to the Machines table in the README: its CPU model
     and microarchitecture. Preflight prints the family/model number to look up.

  Limits that remain:
  - SGX needs that machine to have SGX enabled, the Intel SDK in
    /opt/intel/sgxsdk, and your account in its sgx group. Otherwise you run
    native only and report that limitation, which the assignment explicitly
    allows.
  - Interpretation is per machine. The scripts collect the right data anywhere,
    but what the numbers mean (published ROB/PRF sizes, µop cache size, 32 KiB
    L1i) is specific to each microarchitecture. You'll need to read them for
    that CPU when writing the report.
